Overview of the 3 themes and their 13 subthemes.

### 3.1 Theme 1: An opportunity for exploring self-identity

Firstly, the studies showed that students with disability, no less than their peers, experienced post-secondary education as a period of ongoing identity exploration ([Squires et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref140); [Dangoisse et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref31)). While this might be considered an ‘internal’ issue for students ([Squires and Countermine, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref141)), and therefore not strictly within the scope of our study on how HE institutions are expected to accommodate students, it is linked to our search for considering student aspirations, and also to access and accommodations in two ways: firstly, because this internal struggle is part of the process of students’ readiness to disclose their disability or otherwise with implications for accessing needed accommodations ([Vergunst and Swartz, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref158)); and, secondly, because the identity process also reflects the experience of being accepted and valued as equals at university ([Ule, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref151)). Two studies ([Vaccaro et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref156); [Newman et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref115)) described this process as the development of students’ sense of purpose. It was found that students with disability had varying perspectives on their future, ranging from having no clear sense of purpose to having a purpose beyond the self ([Newman et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref115)). Students with psychosocial disabilities ‘indicated that experiences in college act as catalysts for identity exploration’ ([O'Shea and Kaplan, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref121), p. 366). This process is here described through four subthemes for Theme 1, namely, overcoming stigma, developing resilience and autonomy, the perception of self-advocacy as a crucial transitional skill, and the need for preparation for transition to employment.

#### 3.1.1 Subtheme 1.1: Facing stigma

Many studies reported that students with disability felt different than others. For instance, students with visual impairment reported that, even though they felt respected, ‘it is as if we were living in two completely separate worlds’ (<span id="ref15-button"></span><span id="ref15a"></span>Biggeri et al., 2020, p.918). An inclusive university environment is needed to foster a healthy identity development ([Moriña, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref104)). But peers were observed to have low expectations for people with communication impairments ([Manyumwa, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref93); [Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)) and other disabilities because they ‘confuse disability with low intelligence’ ([Vlachou and Papananou, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref160), p. 216). Students with disability felt inferior to others and avoided use of accommodations that singled them out in an environment that valued normalcy (<span id="ref9-button"></span><span id="ref9a"></span>Almog, 2018; [Squires et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref140); <span id="ref13-button"></span><span id="ref13a"></span>Bê, 2019). Many studies highlighted the challenges of stigma stemming from being different in an ableist environment (e.g., <span id="ref2-button"></span>Abes and Wallace, 2018; [Sarrett, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref135); [Grimes et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref52), [2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref53); [Lett et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref82); <span id="ref3-button"></span>Abrams and Abes, 2021; [Li et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref83)). ‘Stigma’ is a significant issue in 24 studies and is mentioned 783 times across 72 studies. Students with disability may feel undervalued because students without disability tended to adopt the medical model, which pathologises individual differences, while they themselves embraced the social model, which instead attributes difficulties to the ableist social norms (<span id="ref16-button"></span>Bogart et al., 2019). Stigma was felt more intensely due to intersectionality, such as students from marginalised groups, and/or having additional disabilities and mental health conditions ([Thompson-Ebanks and Jarman, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref147)), and/or disadvantaged socio-economic background (<span id="ref2-button"></span>Abes and Wallace, 2018; [Vaccaro et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref156); [Taneja-Johansson, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref145)).

However, two studies focused on the resilient positive identity development of students with disability in a ‘society that has become more open to difference’ ([Ule, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref151), p. 1604). They reported that at university ‘people were more open-minded and expressed less judgement towards them which allowed them to feel more self-confident and develop a sense of belonging towards the university community’ ([Dangoisse et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref31), p. 520).

On the other hand, as accommodations exposed their being different, students ‘needed to balance the perceived benefits of accommodations with their fear of stigmatisation’ ([Dangoisse et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref31), p. 523; [Freedman et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref48)). Because students were ‘anxious and preoccupied with imaginations of what stigma their status could bring’ ([Mngomezulu, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref102), p. 272), some opted not to disclose their disability to their peers and lecturers, forgoing the opportunity for support ([Devar et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref32); <span id="ref10-button"></span><span id="ref10a"></span>Aquino, 2021; [McKinney and Swartz, 2022](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref96)). [Grimes et al. (2019)](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref52) reported that students either did not disclose their disabilities as they felt they were responsible to find solutions, or revealed it when necessary to lecturers but not through official channels, or sought external support instead (i.e., professionals, family and friends). Similarly, the majority of students in [Van Hees et al. (2018)](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref157) decided to mask their difficulties and act like any other student. In a Far Eastern university, no students with mental health conditions and Learning Disability (termed Specific Learning Difficulties—SpLD—in the United Kingdom) offered to participate in the study due to associated stigma ([Yusof et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref164)). Students with mental health conditions found it more difficult to disclose their condition ([Grimes et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref53); [Smith et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref139)), especially students with depression ([Woodhead et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref163)). Students who lacked opportunities for private disclosure experienced more discomfort ([Smith et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref139)). Others feared that disclosure would threaten future employment ([Grimes et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref52); [McKinney and Swartz, 2022](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref96)). The use of the term ‘disability’ prevented a student from seeking support services ([Osborne, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref120)).

#### 3.1.2 Subtheme 1.2: Disability as a resilience factor

Some students also felt that their disability interfered with their goals and options ([Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)), with some claiming that they were denied the opportunity to enrol in a particular course (<span id="ref26-button"></span><span id="ref26a"></span>Cassells and Weber, 2018; [Vlachou and Papananou, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref160); [Francis and Chiu, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref45)). On the other hand, in other studies students talked about perseverance in a field despite experiencing setbacks ([Vaccaro et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref156)), and how disability strengthened their achievement goals ([Francis et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref46); [Newman et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref115)). Additionally, some students with physical disabilities felt more resilient: ‘I think I’ve grown into a more determined person because of my impairment since sometimes I had to fight more for my rights than I would have needed to otherwise’ ([Ule, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref151), p. 1599). Similarly, in [Dangoisse et al. (2020)](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref31), students with physical and sensory disabilities felt better equipped than non-disabled students to handle the new obstacles of university life. Graduates with learning disability underlined ‘that their disabilities had forced them to face all kinds of difficulties, and that these situations had strengthened them and led to their development…: it forced them to take chances, to demand more from themselves…’ ([Russak and Hellwing, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref133), p. 417; [O’Byrne et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref117)).

For some students, disability extended their horizons beyond just deciding about a course or career: ‘It was about doing something meaningful with one’s life. Students often talked about ‘making a difference,’ ‘helping others,’ or ‘paying \[support and advocacy\] forward” ([Vaccaro et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref156), p. 41). ‘After \[multiple spinal\] surgeries I was like, I wanna be a biomedical engineer and I wanna solve all the world’s problems’. (p. 43; see also [Moriña et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref107); [Vlachou and Papananou, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref160)).

#### 3.1.3 Subtheme 1.3: Self-advocacy skills for transition to higher education

Self-advocacy skills were a main focus of eight studies which reported that students saw them as key for successful transition to HE ([Fleming et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref40); [Mask and DePountis, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref94); [Squires and Countermine, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref141); [Kreider et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref74); <span id="ref5-button"></span><span id="ref5a"></span>Accardo et al., 2019b; [Pfeifer et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref128), [2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref127); <span id="ref20-button"></span><span id="ref20a"></span>Bruce and Aylward, 2021). Self-advocacy is mentioned 769 times across 47 studies. Students felt the need to recognise their disability and ‘learn to ask for some help and to get over the fear of disturbing others or being stigmatised’ ([Dangoisse et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref31), p. 520). Students with mental health conditions were found to have lower self-efficacy than those with learning disability ([Jorgensen et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref66)). The latter stated that, ‘Regardless of the source of support, it was very obvious that in order to succeed, the individual must have enough self-awareness and self-confidence to reach out and create the social network’ ([Russak and Hellwing, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref133), p. 419). The same was observed regarding the empowerment of students with physical disabilities ([Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)) and ADHD ([Pfeifer et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref128)).

Students on the autism spectrum felt that transition preparation programmes were most beneficial as they provided opportunities to meet other students, practise navigating the college environment, and develop schedules (<span id="ref5-button"></span><span id="ref5a"></span>Accardo et al., 2019b; [Lei et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref80); [Kim et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref72)). In addition, the transition was an ongoing process ([Hewett et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref59)) that presented more challenges to students with disability and required more effort and need for support ([Pacheco et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref122), [2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref123); [O’Byrne et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref117)). For example, students with sensory impairments had to learn about the university’s academic programme and physical environment and deal with the challenges like finding friends ([Pacheco et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref122); [Hendry et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref57)). Students on the autism spectrum needed help with reducing the heightened anxiety they felt in an unfamiliar situation in addition to academic support (<span id="ref14-button"></span><span id="ref14a"></span>Bell et al., 2017).

#### 3.1.4 Subtheme 1.4: Need to be prepared for employment

Students perceived facilitation into the job market as key for overcoming family dependence as well as societal oppressive norms that saw disability as incompatible with ‘employment, independent living, sexuality and parenthood’ ([Vlachou and Papananou, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref160), p. 552). University was seen as a first step towards developing their autonomy, which would be achieved through finding their preferred job and achieving personal independence ([Clouder et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref28)). They believed that their time in university helped them acquire a variety of life skills, such as taking initiative and communication ([Hadley, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref55); [O’Byrne et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref117); [Vincent, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref159)). On the other hand, some reported lack of preparation for employment (<span id="ref24-button"></span><span id="ref24a"></span>Büscher-Touwen et al., 2018; [Lucas et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref86); [Hewett et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref58); [Lessy et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref81); [Lucas et al., 2022](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref87)). While organisation factors (career counselling and placements) were found to facilitate the transition to work, persons with autism found self-direction still difficult and therefore greatly appreciated the individualised support that was received through caring lecturers and mentors ([Ule, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref151); [Pesonen et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref126)).

### 3.2 Theme 2: Students call for transformation of university systems towards accessible physical, social, and learning environments

As was previously mentioned, students with disability want to participate in all facets of university life ([Murphy, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref111)). The word ‘access\*’, which appeared 2,879 times over 125 studies, was the term most frequently used to describe this inclusive approach. Access was not seen in terms of gaining entry into higher education or physical accessibility, but was perceived in terms of participating equitably in all spheres of higher education, including in the community life and social activities and use of facilities of the institution as well as in teaching and learning. Simply providing individual students with individual accommodations does not satisfy their need to belong: ‘Rather than being seen only as someone who needs care and accommodations, Taylor wished people saw him as a valuable person’ (<span id="ref2-button"></span>Abes and Wallace, 2018, p. 552; [Mutanga, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref112)). [Fleming et al. (2017)](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref40) found that students who felt like they belong also had the highest levels of satisfaction with their university experience.

The students’ call for inclusion in all university activity that were reported in the reviewed studies are described in seven subthemes of Theme 2 below (see [Table 3](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#tab3)): (2.1) physical accessibility to buildings and facilities; (2.2) the implementation of Universal Design for Learning (UDL); (2.3) the use of digital technology; (2.4) better understanding of students with disability by lecturers and peers; (2.5) opportunities for involvement in university social life; (2.6) more available information on university policies, facilities, accommodations and support; and (2.7) better understanding by the disability services officers themselves.

#### 3.2.1 Subtheme 2.1: Call for physical accessibility

Physical disability/impairment was particularly addressed in eight studies ([Ule, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref151); [Moriña et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref107); [Moriña and Morgado, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref108); [Moswela and Mukhopadhyay, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref110); [Ijadunola et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref63); [Úbeda-Colomer et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref150); <span id="ref8-button"></span><span id="ref8a"></span>Al Masa'deh, 2020; [Minotti et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref101)). However, these conditions appear in 23 different articles. Four physical accessibility issues were raised: getting to university, navigating the campus, accessing buildings and classrooms ([Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)), and accessing communication ([Moriña and Morgado, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref108); [García-González et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref49)). Accessibility of spaces is measured not merely in terms of being ‘architecturally accessible’ but in terms of usability: ‘one that is large enough and equipped with the technology necessary for studying is vital for ensuring that students with disability can participate in university life’ (<span id="ref15-button"></span><span id="ref15a"></span>Biggeri et al., 2020, p. 920).

Accessible roads and transportation are key for mobility and independence ([Kunnath and Mathew, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref76); [Francis and Chiu, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref45); [García-González et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref49)), including disabled-friendly buses ([Moriña and Morgado, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref108); [Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)), and pot=hole free and non-muddy streets ([Úbeda-Colomer et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref150); <span id="ref18-button"></span>Braun and Naami, 2021). Students objected to being required to use non-regular access or physical support: ‘I do not feel good about it \[using the entrance for cars\] because I am a human being and I should use the entrance for people and not the one for cars’ (<span id="ref18-button"></span>Braun and Naami, 2021, p. 105).

The locations and timing between lectures were a problem for students with visual and physical impairments, leading to late arrival for sessions (<span id="ref21-button"></span><span id="ref21a"></span>Bualar, 2018; [Yusof et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref164)) and tiredness ([Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)). Students listed inaccessible dorms and generally badly designed buildings ([Duma, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref34); [Francis and Chiu, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref45); [García-González et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref49)), lack of ramps, elevators and automated doors (<span id="ref18-button"></span>Braun and Naami, 2021), and inaccessible library areas, and lecturers’ offices ([Moriña and Morgado, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref108); [Mutanga, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref112)). According to a Greek study, ‘most of the participants talked about unsuitable elevators, stairs and lighting, absence of ramps and of wheelchair accessible toilets as well as classrooms with poor acoustics’ ([Vlachou and Papananou, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref160), p. 213; see also [Yusof et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref164)). The availability of facilities is frequently not sufficient: ‘there are issues like parking designated for the disabled being utilised by others, broken toilets, maintenance problems…’ ([Yusof et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref164), p. 1154); and delayed responses to requests for alternative accessible venues (Mutanga).

There were also issues with how classrooms were set-up: desks and seating secured with screws created problems ([Moriña and Perera, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref109)), including for participation in activities that required students to switch seats ([Osborne, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref120)). Blind students had difficulty using their braille slate on small tables (<span id="ref21-button"></span><span id="ref21a"></span>Bualar, 2018; [Melero et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref99)).

The need to move around for information was coupled with a lack of availability or inadequacy of alternate electronic communication ([García-González et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref49)), such as paper copies of forms or complicated and inaccessible websites for applications that blind students could not complete without sighted assistance ([Hewett et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref59)).

These physical access barriers were reported to have a significant negative impact on students’ participation and ‘academic freedom’, such as having to change programmes for better accessibility or missing tutorials (<span id="ref18-button"></span>Braun and Naami, 2021). Such barriers resulted in ‘feelings of exclusion, fear, sadness, pain, shame, isolation, humiliation, agony, discomfort, dejection,… feeling exhausted, tired, beaten, and dispirited… \[and\] infantilised when they had to rely on others to help them move around campus’ (p. 105).

#### 3.2.2 Subtheme 2.2: call for universal design for learning

Universal Design (UD) can be applied to any environment or product in HE (<span id="ref22-button"></span><span id="ref22a"></span>Burgstahler, 2015). But it has been more widely used as an appeal for systemic access to learning termed Universal Design for Instruction ([Scott et al., 2003](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref136)), or Universal Design for Learning (UDL) which universities are offering as part of the resources for faculty and staff ([University of Rochester, n.d.](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref155)). UDL was a main focus of five studies ([Griful-Freixenet et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref51); [Nieminen and Pesonen, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref116); [Yusof et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref164); [Ndlovu, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref114); [Wilkens et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref162)) and was mentioned in 51 studies. This call has greater importance because it addresses the needs of both students with recognised disabilities as well as those of many others with unrecognised needs ([Jansen et al., 2017b](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref65)). Moreover, it helps students with disability not to feel ‘singled out’ or segregated for accommodations ([Hewett et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref60)). UDL suggests that lecturers can encourage diverse students to participate by using multiple means of (i) representation of what needs to be learned, (ii) action and expression (how it will be demonstrated and acquired), and (iii) engagement (motivation for learning; [CAST, n.d.](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref27)). Two studies ([Griful-Freixenet et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref51); [Nieminen and Pesonen, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref116)) examined whether student needs were addressed through UDL. The use of multimedia, text in alternate formats, structured material, and coherent delivery \[‘When instructors structure things clearly then you have a skeleton to hang everything you hear on …’ ([Griful-Freixenet et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref51), p. 1636)\], and the inclusion of fieldwork or real objects were among the UDL representational components that students cited. The use of an online platform, the provision of notes or guidance and templates, outlining clear expectations, using small group exercises and discussions, multiple forms of assessment, including self-assessment practises, as well as the use of various tools, such as a word processor ([Griful-Freixenet et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref51)), and a detailed rubric, were all examples of UDL elements of action and expression ([Nieminen and Pesonen, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref116)). Accessibility issues of online learning were also of concern to both students with disability and those without ([Heiman et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref56); [Gin et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref50); [Wilkens et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref162)).

However, because students had a variety of needs, different accommodations were required. For instance, various learning preferences—such as the combination of sensory inputs, repetition of content, and the level of autonomy and guidance provided by the instructor—were perceived as advantageous by some and as obstacles by others ([Griful-Freixenet et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref51)). While some participants favoured online resources, others preferred printed materials; some learned through lectures, but ‘for some people, sitting through a lecture is a waste of time’ ([Nieminen and Pesonen, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref116), p. 16). Students also varied in their preferred evaluation method.

Moreover, given that most universities adopted a ‘one-size-fits-all’ approach to curriculum design, most participants emphasised the significance of formal accommodations for succeeding in their courses (e.g., [Griful-Freixenet et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref51)). Additionally, some needs went beyond UDL and required individual arrangements such as a blind person’s need for assistance with graphs ([Griful-Freixenet et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref51); <span id="ref15-button"></span><span id="ref15a"></span>Biggeri et al., 2020). One study found that the best way to achieve effective individualised accommodations was through negotiation over time between the student and the lecturer ([Hewett et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref60)). Indeed, one study reported that students with disability felt that their lecturers needed to be able to address their individual learning needs ([Fox and McNally, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref44)).

#### 3.2.3 Subtheme 2.3: call for lecturers to attend to students’ needs

Students valued being part of the university community. The attitudes and actions of lecturers thus hampered or encouraged student engagement ([Vlachou and Papananou, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref160)). Many studies (e.g., [O’Byrne et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref117); <span id="ref3-button"></span>Abrams and Abes, 2021; [García-González et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref49)) revealed significant student anxiety over instructors’ lack of understanding. One-fifth of students in [Osborne (2019)](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref120) ‘specifically mentioned that they wished their academic teaching staff would understand that they are not lazy, and that they are not faking their condition in order to gain marks’ (p. 239; see also [Moriña et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref107)). Students with invisible disabilities had to struggle to be recognised as needing additional help because of the disbelief of academic staff (<span id="ref13-button"></span><span id="ref13a"></span>Bê, 2019; [Kain et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref67); [Osborne, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref120)).

Some lecturers thought the student lacked the necessary aptitude and asked them to change their course of study, while others opposed statements of need because they thought learning disability did not exist, or thought that people with disabilities could not succeed in college ([Squires and Countermine, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref141)), and refused to make the suggested accommodations ([Hewett et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref59); [Langørgen and Magnus, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref79); [Sarrett, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref135); <span id="ref1-button"></span>Abed and Shackelford, 2020; <span id="ref12-button"></span><span id="ref12a"></span>Bartz, 2020; [Moriña and Perera, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref109)). When refusing to give a copy of the lecture presentation ahead of time, a lecturer told the student: ‘Well, buy yourself better glasses’ ([Melero et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref99), p.1134). These attitudes may lead to students not asserting their needs ([Freedman et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref48); [Pfeifer et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref127)). Indeed one student was told by the head of department: ‘You are like everybody else, a student like every other student, so try to be like everyone else’ (<span id="ref9-button"></span><span id="ref9a"></span>Almog, 2018, p.225; [Padilla-Carmona et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref124)). One student had to formally lodge a complaint with student services about a lecturer who kept denying requests to record lectures ([Mamboleo et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref92)). Moreover, both overt discrimination and more subtle ableist microaggressions were found to be related to higher levels of anxiety and depression, as well as to a lower academic self-concept ([Lett et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref82)). Such attitudes thus deny the recognition, enjoyment or exercise, on an equal basis with others, of disabled students’ right to a tertiary education ([UN General Assembly, 2006](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref152)).

The studies highlighted how students felt they had a harder time managing university responsibilities, particularly due to side effects of medication or associated mental health issues, and that this was not acknowledged by lecturers ([Sarrett, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref135); [Squires and Countermine, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref141); [Osborne, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref120)). All of this had an impact on students’ motivation, sense of belonging in the classroom, and confidence in their own capacity for success ([Ehlinger and Ropers, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref37)). Students complained that the university administration did not have appropriate regulations and procedures to ensure their needs were met, or, when these were in place, they were not followed by staff ([Mutanga, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref112); [Stegenga et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref142)).

Not all academics were aware of students’ individual needs, even when pertinent policies existed ([Squires and Countermine, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref141); [Moriña and Perera, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref109); [García-González et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref49)). Students claimed that the university lacked a process for communicating their needs to the appropriate lecturers ([Moriña et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref106); [Squires and Countermine, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref141)). Students highlighted the need for initial meetings with lecturers early in the semester ([Mamboleo et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref92)). However, García-González et al. claimed that lecturers were unable to meet with the students.

On the other hand, others spoke of how their learning and participation were facilitated by lecturers who were open-minded, attentive, and truly concerned about their needs ([Langørgen and Magnus, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref79); [Melero et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref99); [Vlachou and Papananou, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref160); <span id="ref13-button"></span><span id="ref13a"></span>Bê, 2019; [Francis et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref46); [Kain et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref67); <span id="ref15-button"></span><span id="ref15a"></span>Biggeri et al., 2020; [Ehlinger and Ropers, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref37); [Frank et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref47); [Mamboleo et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref92)). A visually impaired student described how a lecturer ‘figured out a way to stream what he was putting on the screen to my laptop so I could see it up close and be able to read the documents’ ([Mamboleo et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref92), p. 47); other lecturers expressed openness and concern for students’ well-being and learning, reaching out, sharing resources, and encouraging students’ ongoing engagement in the course after noticing the students’ lack of engagement. Students also spoke in favour of participatory and active classes ([Morgado Camacho et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref103)). They felt most fulfilled when they could contribute to everyone’s learning: ‘I really feel proud and happy when my group is helped by me’; ‘to know that our opinions are important to lecturers for assessment makes me feel so good about learning’ ([Kaur et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref68), p. 765).

Students in 45 of the studies requested staff training on disability issues because of the aforementioned challenges (e.g., [Morgado Camacho et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref103); [Ule, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref151); [Khalifa et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref70); [Melero et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref99); [Squires and Countermine, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref141); [Flink and Leonard, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref41); [Osborne, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref120); [Perera-Rodríguez and Moriña Díez, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref125); <span id="ref4-button"></span>Accardo et al., 2019a; <span id="ref1-button"></span>Abed and Shackelford, 2020; [Meluch and Starcher, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref100); [Moriña and Perera, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref109); [García-González et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref49); [Polo Sánchez and Aparicio Puerta, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref129); [Zabeli et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref165)). Such training could cover topics relating to disabilities generally as well as to particular conditions ([Moriña et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref106); [Sarrett, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref135)). There was also a call for lecturers to receive training in technological applications, such as how to assist blind students ([Perera-Rodríguez and Moriña Díez, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref125)). A hearing-impaired student argued how it is impossible to lip read when lecturers are looking at the blackboard, keep talking while walking, when they are sitting down behind a computer, or when they switch off the lights to read the slides ([Melero et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref99)).

#### 3.2.4 Subtheme 2.4: digital technology experienced as a helpful support

Although students greatly valued the chance to interact with lecturers and peers in classrooms and campuses, they also reported that the addition of online communication and learning opportunities could make their attendance at lectures and learning more easily accessible. Such explicit comparisons occurred in seven studies that captured the COVID-19 experience (one published in 2020 and six in 2021; e.g., [Kourea et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref73)). However, 35 made reference to ‘assistive technology’, 44 used the term ‘digital’, 13 referred to online courses, and 70 studies included ‘email’, and 31 studies mentioned ‘electronic’ contact and materials.

At least two studies reported that students with disability saw the abrupt and poorly planned shift to online learning during the COVID-19 as stressful and unsuccessful ([Kourea et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref73)) leading to loss of accommodations ([Gin et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref50)). Difficulties were reported when the recording was of poor quality, when students with disability were unable to participate in discussions and ask questions, and when the screen was cluttered ([Kent et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref69)). One study, however, found that students with disability coped with it better than other students as they benefited from the use of blended teaching, digital technology, and electronic communication ([Wilkens et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref162)). [Pacheco et al. (2021)](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref123) reported that students felt a sense of mastery and satisfaction when they managed to complete a research essay independently using digital technologies. These technologies aided in planning and organisation, especially time management, and made it easier to access course materials ([Ndlovu, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref114); [Seale et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref137)). Moreover, when students’ assistive technology (AT) needs were met, even their social engagement during lectures and beyond was significantly enhanced ([McNicholl et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref98)).

To manage transition, a variety of technologies were used, including wireless internet connections, mobile phones to save information being discussed in meetings, electronic magnifiers ([Pacheco et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref122)), digital voice recorders and cameras ([Pacheco et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref123)), the campus map in pdf format or Google Maps to navigate through the university environment, and devices to access bus timetables ([Pacheco et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref122)). Additionally, students used the internet to search for information about the university ([Dangoisse et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref31)). They also used internet communication with university employees, and emails to request course information and disability support, as well as for academic materials ([Pacheco et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref122); [Dangoisse et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref31)). Technology helped to overcome constraints like time and place to establish and sustain social relationships, in one instance by the setting up by a lecturer of closed online social groups ([Pacheco et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref122)).

#### 3.2.5 Subtheme 2.5: students want to be part of higher education community

Students with a disability were concerned also about barriers to participating in university life ([Ehlinger and Ropers, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref37); [Lei et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref80); [Hendry et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref57)). Students’ formal and informal interaction with peers and lecturers had important influences on their sense of belonging, self-identity and self-worth ([O'Shea and Kaplan, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref121)). When students had the chance to interact with others, often through extracurricular activities, they performed better academically ([Fleming et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref40); [Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)). They saw that having a strong social network—family, friends, classmates, teachers, coaches, mentors, and support service providers—was essential for success ([Mutanga, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref112); [Russak and Hellwing, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref133)). Students on the autism spectrum ([Sarrett, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref135)) noted that the university lacked accommodations that addressed social needs including access to mentors or a support group for people with disabilities.

Students valued lecturers who fostered a sense of community: ‘working together as a team helped them form a bond between each other… the collegial work environment fostered mutual understanding and respect between the students’ ([Kaur et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref68), p. 764). ‘When the classroom climate is not so structured and when people are able to be more open and vulnerable in that space so that we can learn and bounce ideas off of each other, that’s when my anxiety becomes a lot more manageable’ ([Ehlinger and Ropers, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref37), p. 341). Mentoring was also seen to have a significant impact on engagement ([Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)).

Different accommodations were needed for various disabilities. Socialising was particularly difficult for students with hearing impairment, who struggled to develop alternate forms of communication ([Hendry et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref57)). One student advised that other students study sign language. A blind student heard about forthcoming events but was unable to read any flyers or posters about them ([Mask and DePountis, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref94); [Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)). One study reported that students did not participate in activities on campus as they were discouraged by others, due to lack of physical accessibility, and due to feeling overwhelmed ([Fox et al., 2022](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref43)); one student felt self-conscious about her clubfoot ([Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)). Having a chronic illness, such as lupus, can also make it difficult for students to participate in extracurricular activities (<span id="ref7-button"></span><span id="ref7a"></span>Agarwal and Kumar, 2017).

Studies reported two perspectives for encouraging student engagement in the social life of the university: enabling students with disability to join the general student population or creating safe spaces and self-help groups for people with specific conditions. Students with physical disabilities in [Minotti et al. (2021)](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref101) held both points of view: while some saw the need for a supportive disability community, many did not feel like they belonged to the larger disability community. Similarly, some students with autism felt separate spaces can feel safe and inclusive, but at the same time interacting with the wider university community promoted independence ([Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)). Students with mental health conditions felt the need to discuss their struggles with people who had gone through a similar situation, but they also believed that by doing so, they were rendered invisible to the campus community and missing the opportunity to renegotiate their identity ([O'Shea and Kaplan, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref121)). An argument for self-advocacy groups was made regarding students with Lupus (<span id="ref7-button"></span><span id="ref7a"></span>Agarwal and Kumar, 2017) as well as for student teachers with visual or physical disability ([Subrayen and Suknunan, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref143)). Some students claimed that they combined the two perspectives by becoming activists for inclusion ([Mays and Brevetti, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref95)).

#### 3.2.6 Subtheme 2.6: need for students to be informed

Another systemic need identified by studies was for them to be given more information on two levels: first, about university architecture, practises, and organisational aspects generally, and whether or not these were accessible (e.g., <span id="ref8-button"></span><span id="ref8a"></span>Al Masa'deh, 2020); and second, about the accommodations that were available and how to access them (e.g., [Langørgen and Magnus, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref79); [Melero et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref99); <span id="ref13-button"></span><span id="ref13a"></span>Bê, 2019; [Flink and Leonard, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref41); [Francis et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref46); [Ijadunola et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref63)). The first academic year was particularly difficult for students as they were unaware of accommodations, and they felt lost and dependent on the staff’s goodwill ([Moriña and Perera, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref109); [Pfeifer et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref128); [Dreyer, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref33)). Lack of knowledge of accommodations available was also reported for online courses ([Encuentra and Gregori, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref39)).

Students in [Van Hees et al. (2018)](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref157) stressed the importance of having a point of contact who can orient them to their new environments, inform them about activities, advise them on socially appropriate behaviour, and be someone they can turn to for help. This point of contact could introduce them to faculty members and other personnel ([Murphy, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref111)).

#### 3.2.7 Subtheme 2.7: need for disability support service to be more effective

Studies described how students valued the support of the DSS but also pointed out its inadequacies, particularly as a ‘bureaucratic jungle’ ([Langørgen and Magnus, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref79); [Moriña and Perera, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref109); [Kim and Crowley, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref71)). These experiences differed at various universities as well. Some students believed that the DSS itself either lacked a proper understanding of the students’ needs (visual impairment; [Hewett et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref59)), or the authority and resources necessary to efficiently organise support plans ([Vlachou and Papananou, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref160)). Some reported the lack of guidelines for accessing support and that the procedures requiring a formal diagnosis and its processing as too onerous, so that students spent months waiting to be tested and to receive approval, and to get finally get the necessary accommodation (<span id="ref1-button"></span>Abed and Shackelford, 2020).

In one study students reported that the DSS counsellor was not advocating sufficiently for students ([Mamboleo et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref92)). However, other students believed that the DSS was trying its best to assist students who encountered difficulties and was especially helpful in providing laptops with voice software ([Vlachou and Papananou, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref160)), or in organising Individual Support Plans ([Francis and Chiu, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref45)).

Parents were at times involved with the DSS. While parental support was reported as a success factor for students with disability ([Moriña and Biagiotti, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref105)), students had various opinions regarding parent involvement. The right to self-determination, autonomy, and expression was clamoured for by some. Others also opposed uninvited parental participation in activities (e.g., [Van Hees et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref157); [Duma and Shawa, 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref35)). On the other hand, some students requested parental support while making curriculum decisions or speaking with staff to learn more about access arrangements, among other things. Additionally, students felt that their parents supported them emotionally throughout challenging and transitional moments ([Van Hees et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref157); [Francis et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref46)). Visually impaired students also valued the guidance given by family members regarding choice of institution ([Pacheco et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref123)).

### 3.3 Theme 3: students find course and examination accommodations very helpful

As was said above, because of stigma, students with disability were frequently hesitant to request accommodations. They highlighted the importance of not using deficit language in official forms and asked for more specific categories that they did not associate with disability ([Grimes et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref52)). Moreover, students themselves might face the dilemma of needing to develop autonomy, also in preparation for employment, while having a pressing need for support to overcome difficulties in completing assignments ([Hadley, 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref54)). Thus, some did not request accommodations to assert their independence: ‘I want to go through college and succeed without Disability Services’ ([Squires et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref140)). However, most students said that they sorely needed individual course and test access arrangements to create a fair playing field for them ([Sarrett, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref135)). ‘Accommod\*’ was mentioned 3,087 times in 113 of the studies. Students called for more effective processing of such accommodations. Accommodations boost students’ self-confidence and give students with disability equal opportunities to succeed while learning alongside their peers (<span id="ref1-button"></span>Abed and Shackelford, 2020).

Students also mentioned a number of difficulties that made it harder to implement accommodations. These included the instructor’s lack of understanding, their judgmental attitudes, improper implementation of accommodations, and the DSS counsellor not advocating sufficiently for students (<span id="ref1-button"></span>Abed and Shackelford, 2020; [Freedman et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref48); [Mamboleo et al., 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref92)). Delays in getting the accommodations or equipment required were reported in several studies (e.g., [Fossey et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref42); [Langørgen and Magnus, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref79); [Stegenga et al., 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref142)). Some students received assistance when they terminated their studies ([Moriña and Perera, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref109)). Students were worried about lagging behind without support ([Lambe et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref77)). Financial difficulties were another issue raised by students with learning disability when trying to have a professional psychological evaluation ([Lambert and Dryer, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref78); [Grimes et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref52)). Others chose not to request any accommodations since the office was ‘slow and disorganised’ ([Squires and Countermine, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref141)).

While many studies reported how students were wary not to be seen as having an advantage through accommodations, one article queried this possibility in private universities that serve students from higher socioeconomic groups: students were found to request accommodations even if they did not have an evidence-based diagnosis of a learning disability ([Weis et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref161)). The authors also point out that most of the top-ranked private liberal arts colleges in the United States have double to triple the national average percentage of students receiving accommodations for disabilities.

Thus, [Weis et al. (2017)](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref161) call for evidence-based documentation of needs, which was indeed what studies showed was usually required. While some students reported starting to understand themselves better and felt validated after receiving a diagnosis ([Francis et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref46)), there are also questions about the requirements for self-disclosure, bureaucracy, and expenses involved in such a ‘medical’ approach ([Griful-Freixenet et al., 2017](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref51)).

#### 3.3.1 Subtheme 3.1: common and varied accommodations for different difficulties

Students with a variety of conditions and in various institutions used typically similar accommodations for both coursework and assessment (note that we have not included here accommodations for community engagement, but such individualised support for engagement is evident in the search for mentoring as explained below). Moreover, while studies frequently listed accommodations by type of disability, [Table 4](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#tab4) presents a collection of accommodations categorised by need, namely, physical difficulties, medical needs, sensory processing difficulties, hearing impairment, visual impairment, executive functioning difficulties, self-regulation and social difficulties. This categorisation is more appropriate because students highlighted the need for individualised accommodations based on need rather than category of condition ([Hsiao et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/education/articles/10.3389/feduc.2023.1218120/full#ref62); <span id="ref4-button"></span>Accardo et al., 2019a).
